## Gender policy

### The AEI provided a gender assessment and action plan and therefore complies with the GCF standard on gender policy. The AEI has identified that the most significant risks to achieving gender equality are those related to the lack of equal access to resources and opportunities for women and girls, particularly in rural areas. Climate change further exacerbates these challenges by increasing vulnerability and reducing resilience. These issues affect both men and women differently, with women often facing greater barriers to accessing services and resources due to their roles within households and communities. The AEI has identified the following risks:

- Lack of access to credit
- Limited access to land and property rights
- Limited access to education and training
- Limited access to health care

The program best gender outcome (GP) includes a set of gender-related activities that will be implemented in order to achieve these outcomes. The AEI has a robust structural framework for the implementation of its gender policy, including:

- Gender mainstreaming indicators
- Gender equality objectives
- Gender equality targets

# Green Climate Fund

## Operational risks

### a) Compliance risk (mission risk)

The programme’s approved activities, with their focus on land restoration, addressing climate change and sustainable development, are subject to compliance requirements. Non-compliance may result in reputational damage, financial penalties or even the suspension of funding. The programme will be the first multilateral country-led initiative to promote large-scale ecosystem restoration as a means to address climate change. This will require the establishment of robust monitoring systems to ensure that activities are implemented in accordance with approved procedures and standards.

### b) Financial risk

The programme’s financial risk is considered low due to the following reasons:

- The programme has established strong partnerships with high-level international country governments, as well as local governments and other stakeholders.
- The programme has a robust monitoring system in place that ensures compliance with approved procedures and standards.
- The programme has a strong governance structure that includes regular oversight by an independent advisory board.

### c) Consideration of time and scope of the multi-country pilot programme

The programme will be implemented over a period of five years, including large-scale activities and capacity-building at various levels of government. This will require significant resources to ensure effective implementation and monitoring.

In the event of approved, the impact of this proposal on the GCF conservation risk is considered low due to the following reasons:

- The programme has a strong governance structure that includes regular oversight by an independent advisory board.

# 4.4. Fiduciary

1. The fiduciary framework of the RIO+20/GSCF programme is structured to ensure strong financial integrity, effective verification and accountability across all levels of implementation. This includes a robust system of internal controls and audit mechanisms that are designed to provide an assurance of accuracy. FAD will annually audit all approved fiduciary arrangements for GCP projects to ensure compliance with the programme's requirements. The audits will be conducted by independent external auditors, and the results will be reported to the Programme Board. The framework also includes a system for regular reporting on financial integrity and transparency, which is overseen by an internal audit function.

2. The programme will be implemented through standardised governance and delivery arrangements, a rigorous procurement process, transparent budgeting, programming, monitoring and evaluation systems, and robust risk management frameworks. This will ensure that FAD country offices adhere to fiduciary standards for all approved GCP agreements, with FAD supported by national governments. FAD and selected partners through agreed modalities that strengthen governance arrangements.

3. Strong governance and oversight will be provided by a regional programme steering committee, chaired by the Africa Climate Change Committee, and national steering committees in each country. These committees will include representatives from key government departments, civil society organisations and other relevant stakeholders. The Fiduciary Framework will also include an independent external audit function to ensure compliance with fiduciary standards.

4. GCP projects will be monitored by FAD and partners to ensure that national governments are effectively implementing the programme. This includes regular reporting on financial integrity and transparency, which is overseen by an internal audit function. The framework also includes a system for regular reporting on financial integrity and transparency, which is overseen by an independent external audit function.

# 4.5. Results monitoring and reporting

The monitoring and reporting results will be standardised pathway outputs NITI and GCP projects. This will include the use of standard indicators to measure progress against agreed outcomes. The pathfinding strategy will be implemented through the logical framework approach, with regular updates on progress reported to the Programme Board over the 10-year period.

The results will be consolidated into three GCP results areas: namely delivery and impact for mitigation, and the most vulnerable people and communities and ecosystems and ecosystem services. This includes a focus on reducing greenhouse gas emissions by 30% (equivalent to approximately 60 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent).

# Green Climate Fund

## GCF-014/CLIMA/2018

### a) Core indicators: the number of direct (1.08 million) and indirect (3.16 billion) beneficiaries reached.

### b) The number of direct (1.75 million) and indirect (1.71 million) beneficiaries reached.

### c) Supplementary indicators: the number of households adopting climate-resilient agriculture practices, the number of hectares under sustainable land management, and the number of hectares under improved water management.

These indicators are well integrated into the logical framework with appropriate baselines.

The monitoring and evaluation plan is comprehensive; as indicated it is based on the following principles: 1) inclusion of provisions for coordination, reporting, and validation that align with the GCF’s M&E system; 2) alignment with the Paris Agreement and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs); 3) use of relevant quality standards; the application of gender equality and social inclusion considerations; and 4) alignment with the National Adaptation Plan (NAP).

As this is a multi-country programme, the funding proposal integrates the appropriate indicators to measure the impact of the project. The indicators are aligned with the national indicators for each country and include country-specific targets for key indicators and additional monitoring to collecting and reporting on common global data against the agreed indicators. This will enable the collection of comparable data across countries, enabling learning and experience sharing among countries. Indicators have been developed for regular programming and will be critical in ensuring transparency, accountability and effectiveness.

The M&E system is adequately resourced with a total allocation of USD 11.4 million for the entire programme period. The Secretariat considers the proposed M&E system to be adequate to meet the objectives of the programme. M&E systems are an important part of the programme design and will enable appropriate programming, implementation and reporting on the progress towards the programme objectives.

### 4.6 Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on July 2018 the “AMA” and has been effective as of October 2018. The Accredited Entity has prepared a legal opinion that confirms compliance with the requirements of the GCF’s Accreditation Framework, including the rules of procedures for accreditation, the criteria for accreditation, and the terms and conditions set out in the AMA. The Accredited Entity has also provided a legal opinion to confirm that it is 100% owned by the Government of the Republic of the Philippines.

The Accredited Entity has provided a certificate confirming that it has satisfied all requirements under the GCF’s Accreditation Framework and that it is in compliance with the AMA. The Accredited Entity has also provided a legal opinion to confirm that it is 100% owned by the Government of the Republic of the Philippines.

The proposed programme will be implemented in eight host countries. The GCF has approved the Accredited Entity’s request for an extension of the Secretariat’s approval of the Accreditation Master Agreement to include the Philippines as a host country.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP268

## Proposed name
Scaling Up Beneficial Insects in Africa’s Great Green Wall (SUBIA-GGW)

## Accepted acronym
Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

## Countries (proposed)
Benin, Burkina Faso, Chad, Ethiopia, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria, Senegal

## Proposed/preparatory scale
Medium

## L. Assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

The proposal was submitted to the Secretariat on 15 December 2020. It aims at contributing to sustainable agricultural development and food security in Africa by promoting the use of beneficial insects as biological control agents against pests. The project will be implemented over a period of five years (2021-2026) with an annual budget of USD 3 million.

The submission contains all highly relevant climate change and other adaptive benefits as stated by the proponent. The proposed programme is “Scaling Up Beneficial Insects in Africa’s Great Green Wall” (SUBIA-GGW). The project will be implemented through a series of activities including:

- Establishing 10 demonstration sites for biological control;
- Training 20 national extension workers and creating 10 regional training centers to support the scaling up of biological control;
- Developing and disseminating guidelines on biological control;
- Promoting the use of biological control agents in integrated pest management (IPM);
- Enhancing the capacity of farmers, extension workers and other stakeholders.

# Impact Potential

## Scale: Medium to High

The emerging project contributes to both mitigation and adaptation benefits.

### Greenhouse gas (GHG) sequestration and reduction emissions are achieved through land-use management and large-scale restoration of degraded biomes and ecosystems.

- **Restoration** of 1 million hectares of degraded lands by 2030.
- **Reforestation** of 50,000 hectares of degraded forests annually.
- **Afforestation** of 100,000 hectares of degraded grasslands annually.
- **Conservation** of 500,000 hectares of existing native forests.

### Beyond mitigation of global climate change, the project also provides multiple co-benefits.

- **Biodiversity**: Enhances habitat for endangered species and supports ecosystem services.
- **Water**: Improves water quality and quantity through increased infiltration and reduced runoff.
- **Soil**: Increases soil carbon stocks and improves soil health.
- **Community**: Provides economic benefits to local communities through job creation and sustainable resource use.

### The largest co-benefits are improved conservation, sustainably managed landscapes, and enhanced ecosystem services.

- **Conservation**: Protects biodiversity and maintains ecosystem functions.
- **Landscapes**: Integrates multiple land uses and enhances landscape connectivity.
- **Ecosystem Services**: Provides clean water, air quality improvements, and changing climate patterns.

This will result in significant co-benefits for local communities, including improved livelihoods, enhanced food security, and sustainable resource use. These benefits are achieved through integrated land-use planning, community engagement, and sustainable management practices.

### SUBAAGORA is an innovative new methodology that integrates NTFPs and related increased income from non-timber forest products (NTFPs).

- **Incomes**: Increases household incomes.
- **Livelihoods**: Enhances livelihood security.
- **Communities**: Empowers local communities to manage their resources sustainably.

### The project has generated interest regarding the potentially unequal opportunities in access and benefits from NTFPs.

- **Alliances**: Forms strategic alliances with local communities, NGOs, and government agencies.
- **Partnerships**: Develops partnerships with local and international organizations.
- **Communities**: Engages communities to ensure equitable distribution of benefits.

The project aims to address these challenges by ensuring that the benefits from NTFPs are shared equally among all stakeholders. This will be achieved through community engagement, capacity building, and sustainable resource management practices.

### The project contributes to several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).

- **SDG 1**: End poverty in all its forms everywhere.
- **SDG 2**: End hunger, achieve food security and improved nutrition, and promote sustainable agriculture.
- **SDG 3**: Ensure healthy lives and promote well-being for all at all ages.
- **SDG 8**: Promote sustained, inclusive, and sustainable economic growth, full and productive employment and decent work for all.
- **SDG 13**: Take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts.

The project also contributes to several other SDGs, including those related to land, water, energy, and partnerships. By addressing these goals, the project aims to promote sustainable development and improve the lives of people in the region.

# Green Growth Institute

## National policies and measures and their associated benefits and institutions (see Section B detailed remarks)

The Green Growth Institute will provide self help tools with a standardized set of templates and best practices that will support governments in the monitoring and reporting of the benefits that green growth can bring. This will enable countries to monitor progress towards their nationally determined contributions, making a significant contribution towards carbon sequestration and natural capital development.

### 1.2. Paradigm shift potential

#### Scale: High

- With the shift in focus of the Green Growth Institute from free planning to free planning and implementation, the Institute can play an important role in supporting countries to achieve their nationally determined contributions (NDCs) and sustainable development goals.
- The Institute will support countries to integrate green growth into national policies and strategies, and to develop and implement green growth plans that are integrated with other relevant policies. This will enable countries to achieve their NDCs and SDGs more effectively.

#### Key benefits

- The Institute can play a key role in supporting countries to achieve their NDCs and SDGs by providing technical assistance and capacity building.
- The Institute can support countries to develop and implement green growth plans that are integrated with other relevant policies. This will enable countries to achieve their NDCs and SDGs more effectively.

# Food security, open conflict step by step integrated for example of household stay away fields with standing crops

Food insecurity is related to poverty reduction and sustainability in the new Millennium development goals. Insecurity food is expected to increase food prices and cause panic among farmers. The government has been working on a number of measures to address this issue, including the provision of food aid and the implementation of agricultural policies.

## The impact of climate change on food security

Climate change is one of the most pressing issues facing the world today. It is expected to have significant impacts on food security, particularly in developing countries. In order to mitigate these impacts, it is important to understand the relationship between climate change and food security.

### The role of agriculture in food security

Agriculture plays a crucial role in food security. It provides the majority of the world's food supply and is an important source of income for many people. However, climate change is expected to have significant impacts on agriculture, including changes in temperature, precipitation patterns, and the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events.

### The impact of conflict on food security

Conflict can also have a significant impact on food security. In areas where there is ongoing conflict, it can be difficult for farmers to access their fields and plant crops. Additionally, conflict can disrupt supply chains and lead to shortages of food in affected areas.

## The role of the international community in addressing food insecurity

The international community has an important role to play in addressing food insecurity. In particular, the United Nations Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) is working to address this issue through a variety of initiatives, including the provision of food aid and the implementation of agricultural policies.

### The importance of early warning systems

Early warning systems are crucial for addressing food insecurity. They allow governments and international organizations to anticipate and respond to potential food crises before they become severe. In order to be effective, these systems must be based on accurate and timely data.

### The need for integrated approaches

Addressing food insecurity requires an integrated approach that takes into account the complex interplay of factors that contribute to it. This includes addressing the root causes of poverty, improving access to markets, and promoting sustainable agricultural practices.

## The importance of research and innovation

Research and innovation are essential for addressing food insecurity. In particular, there is a need for more investment in agricultural research and development, as well as in the development of new technologies that can improve the efficiency and sustainability of food production.

### The role of public-private partnerships

Public-private partnerships can play an important role in addressing food insecurity. By bringing together the resources and expertise of both the public and private sectors, these partnerships can help to develop and implement innovative solutions to food security challenges.

### The need for policy coherence

Policy coherence is essential for addressing food insecurity. In particular, there is a need for policies that promote sustainable agricultural practices, improve access to markets, and support the development of rural infrastructure.

## Conclusion

Food insecurity is a complex and pressing issue that requires an integrated approach. By working together, governments, international organizations, and other stakeholders can address the root causes of food insecurity and promote sustainable solutions.

# Green Growth Fund

## Overview of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

### Key Points

- The independent technical advisory panel (ITAP) is a critical component of the Green Growth Fund (GGF).
- ITAP provides strategic oversight and ensures that GGF investments are aligned with the fund's objectives.
- ITAP members include experts in climate change, green growth, and sustainable development.

### Objectives

1. **Review Investment Proposals:**
   - Evaluate proposals for alignment with GGF objectives.
   - Provide recommendations to the GGF Secretariat on investment approvals.

2. **Ensure Technical Quality:**
   - Assess technical quality of proposed investments.
   - Ensure investments meet environmental and social safeguards.

3. **Provide Strategic Guidance:**
   - Offer strategic guidance to the GGF Secretariat.
   - Help shape the fund's investment portfolio.

### Recommendations

- ITAP recommends that the board approve the funding proposal.
- ITAP also recommends to the AF that it invest more in knowledge development in dynamic sectors and long-use capital, particularly in competition with other funds.

## Overall Remarks from the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

### Key Points

- The programmatic stands to become an important contributor to the Green Growth Wall of Success.
- ITAP recommends that the board approve the funding proposal.
- ITAP also recommends to the AF that it invest more in knowledge development in dynamic sectors and long-use capital, particularly in competition with other funds.

1. **Review Investment Proposals:**
   - Evaluate proposals for alignment with GGF objectives.

3. **Provide Strategic Guidance:**
   - Offer strategic guidance to the GGF Secretariat.

### Dynamics of Investment

- ITAP recommends that the board approve the funding proposal.
- ITAP also recommends to the AF that it invest more in knowledge development in dynamic sectors and long-use capital, particularly in competition with other funds.

# Part One

## Executive Summary

Women in the third world are disproportionately affected by increasing food insecurity, promoted crisis, disease and death. They constitute a large proportion of the agricultural labour force in the third world; at the same time, they continue to bear most of the burden of child care and domestic work. Women's participation in agriculture ranges from 60% to 80% of the total agricultural workforce. In sub-Saharan Africa, women account for 75-90% of food production, crop processing, marketing and domestic chores.

### Disadvantages and Inequality

The key gender indicators in the third world show that the disadvantages affecting women within the family range from 65% to 85%. Based on factors such as legal frameworks to control women's mobility, access to education, health care and employment opportunities, women are disadvantaged. Women have less access to land, credit and other productive resources than men. This suggests a lack of self-sufficiency and home-based employment. Women's capacity to decide about their own behaviour is limited by the gender gap in terms of education, knowledge, and access to information. Women's economic opportunities and independence are constrained by cultural norms and practices.

### Representation and Decision-Making Power

Women continue to have a minor representation in decision-making and leadership positions in the public arena. The lack of women's representation in political life is due to the persistence of traditional attitudes towards women, which limit their participation in politics. Women's political participation is limited by the most common forms of gender inequality, such as unequal access to education, health care and economic opportunities.

### Access to Resources

Women face the greatest challenges in accessing productive resources although they are the primary users of these resources. Women have less access to land, credit and other productive resources than men. Women's access to resources is limited by cultural norms that restrict their mobility and access to information. Women's access to resources is also limited by their lack of control over productive resources.

Women's access to resources is also limited by their lack of control over productive resources.

## Legal Frameworks and Enabling Environment

There has been an improvement in conditions over time that have facilitated the development of legal frameworks to address climate change issues. These improvements include the establishment of national environmental agencies, the enactment of legislation, and the creation of institutions to manage and enforce these laws. However, despite these legal improvements, women in the Sahel region continue to face significant challenges due to gender inequalities, poverty, and lack of access to resources. These challenges are exacerbated by household chores and care responsibilities that limit their ability to participate in decision-making processes related to climate change adaptation and mitigation.

## Vulnerability of Climate and Gender/Specific Climate Impacts

The vulnerable groups include women who are responsible for food security, nutrition, water, sanitation, and health services. Women's vulnerability is exacerbated by the lack of access to resources such as land, credit, and education. The impact of climate change on women is compounded by their limited ability to adapt due to social, economic, and cultural factors. For example, in many rural households, men control patterns of resource use and decision-making processes related to climate change adaptation. Also, women are often excluded from participating in decision-making processes that affect their lives.

Women's vulnerability is further exacerbated by the lack of access to resources such as land, credit, education, and health services. Women are also affected by the lack of access to social protection programs and other forms of support. In addition, women are often excluded from participating in climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts due to their limited access to information and resources.

## All of the above circumstances hinder women's ability to meet in sustainable agricultural practices, diversity their income, and build resilience to climate shocks

The limited access to agricultural services and inputs, as well as the lack of financial support, hinders women's ability to diversify their livelihoods. The lack of access to credit and other forms of financial support also limits women's ability to invest in sustainable agricultural practices.

Women and girls bear a disproportionate burden of the impacts when labor is applied to limited or unavailable. This becomes even more significant in agricultural and food production, where women are often responsible for planting, harvesting, and processing crops. Women are also affected by the lack of access to resources such as land, credit, education, and health services. The lack of access to these resources limits women's ability to participate in decision-making processes related to climate change adaptation and mitigation.

Women's vulnerability is further exacerbated by the lack of access to resources such as land, credit, education, and health services. Women are also affected by the lack of access to social protection programs and other forms of support. In addition, women are often excluded from participating in climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts due to their limited access to information and resources.

## Conclusion

Including women in land restoration processes provides multiple benefits such as increased resilience to natural disasters, improved livelihoods, and enhanced food security. Women's ability to own, control, benefit from, and access land in the Sahel region is critical for building resilience to climate change.

from the World Bank show that percentage of land owned by women is always smaller than men in the same countries, women’s access to, use and control of land depends on their relations to male relatives or husbands, and on the prevailing socio-economic conditions. ### SURAGGWA Overview

The eight countries of the Great Green Wall (GGW) are among the world’s poorest and most vulnerable to climate change, with women and girls disproportionately affected. These countries include Burkina Faso, Chad, Ethiopia, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria, and Senegal. The GGW initiative aims to restore 100 million hectares of degraded land by 2030 through sustainable land management practices, such as agroforestry, soil conservation, and integrated water resource management. Extreme climate events in each country have exacerbated the challenges faced by women and girls, including displacement due to conflict and natural disasters.

The degradation of ecosystems and disruption of agriculture and biodiversity has undermined livelihoods, disproportionately affecting women, and increased gender inequality. This has severely impacted women’s ability to access resources and participate in decision-making processes related to the sustainable use of land.

The Stand Up for Her Initiative in Africa’s Great Green Wall (SURAGGWA) programme aims to achieve the objectives of the GGW, an African-led initiative to promote the contribution from developing countries to global efforts to restore degraded lands. The SURAGGWA programme focuses only on the planning but also holistic development in the areas, addressing the unique needs and opportunities of women and girls. SURAGGWA will facilitate a multi-partner, unified approach to address gender equality and women’s empowerment across Africa, including Burkina Faso, Chad, Ethiopia, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria, and Senegal.

The programme will build capacities for coordination, planning, monitoring, medication of financial resources, and capacity building. The programme is supported by the African Development Bank (AfDB), the Global Environment Facility (GEF), the World Bank, and other partners.

SURAGGWA focuses on three key interventions designed to address the technical, social, and economic challenges that women face in restoring degraded lands:

- Develop a programme to scale-up successful restoration practices, promote sustainability, and enhance gender equality.


---
**Page 185**
---

- Support the development of climate-resilient, low-emission non-timber forest product value chains and sustainable management of forests for conservation and ecosystem services.
- Strengthen the Green Climate Fund to expand and enhance its ability to support the implementation of robust country-driven adaptation plans and programs.

> *For full programme description, please refer to the funding proposal document.*

The programme will work with community organizations to build knowledge and capacity, while strengthening community awareness and capacity in understanding ecological functions of their environment. It will emphasize women's participation and leadership, including training and building skills for women to become leaders in their communities. The program will also support financial institutions and address the specific barriers faced by target groups, including women, youth, indigenous peoples, and persons with disabilities. It will promote gender equality and social inclusion, and ensure that women and men have equal access to resources.

The transformational activities of SURAGGHA will support the implementation of the GCF initiative led by the African Union, aiming to transform landscapes by restoring 100 million hectares, creating 10 million jobs, and sequestering 250 million tons CO2e by 2030. The subprogrammed multi-country project will be implemented in collaboration with the African Development Bank (AfDB), the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) and the World Bank Group (WBG). The program will support the implementation of the Great Green Wall initiative, which aims to restore 10 million hectares of degraded land by 2030. The program will also support the implementation of the Bonn Challenge and associated African Forest Landscape Restoration Initiative, which aims to restore 64 million hectares in the coming decade. In the same vein, the project will support the implementation of the UN Decade on Ecosystem Restoration (2021-2030). # Objectives of the gender assessment

Gender equality is a fundamental requirement for achieving sustainable development and climate resilience. Climate change affects women and men differently due to differences in gender roles, responsibilities, and access to resources. Women are often more dependent on natural resources such as land, forests, and water. In addition, they are responsible for food security and the well-being of their families. Gender equality is essential for sustainable development and climate resilience.

The GCF recognizes the importance of gender equality and women's empowerment in achieving climate change adaptation, mitigation, and overall sustainable development. As a result, GCF integrates gender into its operations, funding, and decision-making processes. The FAO also shares this same view and commitment, as indicated in the FAO Policy on Gender Equality. The FAO Gender Policy on Gender Equality aims to promote gender equality and women's empowerment by addressing economic resources, self-help activities, and local services. This assessment highlights the importance of integrating gender into climate change adaptation and mitigation programs.

Gender Policy, which supports the integration of gender into projects at GO-Affiliated programs, and projects to promote equal access to resources, benefits, and decision-making processes. It also aims to ensure that women and men have equal opportunities for participation in climate change adaptation and mitigation programs, acknowledging that climate change disproportionately affects women and marginalized groups due to existing social and economic inequalities.

This Gender Assessment/Assignment for the GCF programme, "Scaling Up Resilience in Africa's Drylands: Building Drought Resilience in Kenya's Arid and Semi-Arid Lands (ASALs)", was conducted by the FAO. The assessment aims to identify gender-related challenges and opportunities that affect the target communities. The assessment also aims to provide recommendations for addressing these challenges and opportunities.

The assessment was carried out using a mixed-method approach, combining both qualitative and quantitative data collection methods. The assessment included focus group discussions (FGDs), key informant interviews (KIIs), and household surveys. The following chapters contain gender-related issues and recommendations for food security and nutrition resilience and climate change adaptation in the target communities.

The assessment was conducted with support from the FAO Gender Policy, which aims to promote gender equality and women's empowerment by addressing economic resources, self-help activities, and local services. # Plan SAPP: The SAPP Gender Action Plan

The primary objective of the Gender Action Plan is to address gender inequalities in climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts.

1. **Identify** gender disparities in climate resilience, agriculture, and rural livelihoods, highlighting the unique challenges faced by women, men, and other social groups in the Great Green Wall region.
2. **Promote** gender-responsive climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts.
3. **Provide** actionable recommendations to ensure the Lubuko/IDC programme integrates gender equality into its climate resilience activities.

4. **Align** with existing global commitments such as the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) Gender Action Plan and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDG 5 & 13), reinforcing the role of gender equity in climate adaptation efforts.

## Methodology

The methodology is based on a multi-dimensional approach, drawing from:

- **Desk review**
  - Literature on gender, fairness, and climate change + GOGW countries' policies.
  - Gender equality and climate resilience initiatives by the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) and the Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS).
  - Country-level gender profiles.

- **Primary data** collected through field-level consultations, field-work observations, and key informant interviews in the target countries:
  - Focus on agriculture, climate change adaptation, and livelihoods.
  - Consultations with local communities, women's associations, civil society organizations, representatives from government ministries, and non-governmental organizations (NGOs).
- **Secondary data** from the Authority of the GOGW, gender programme teams operating in the area. These consultations described the challenges faced by different groups and their needs for support to enhance understanding of the role of women in the GOGW area and the constraints they face. The consultations also helped to identify key gender issues, vulnerabilities, and capacities that can be addressed through the plan's components and activities.

*LUBUKO/IDC 2020, SAPP Gender Action Plan.*
*Please refer to the eight household country-level gender profiles submitted, prepared by IMO between 2018*

Based on the findings from this gender assessment, a set of recommendations were developed to mainstream gender and the activities in the SURAGGRA program in a way that responds to identified needs.

The main recommendations are:

- **By addressing the Gender Action Learning System (GALS)** to develop the capacity of farmers and other stakeholders.
- **Replacing the related GALS** with a more comprehensive approach that will ensure the implementation of gender-transformative strategies, helping bridge gender gaps in agriculture, enhance women's participation in decision-making, and build confidence among various communities of the great green wall region.

# IV. Regional overview of gender inequalities and the status of women in the Sahel

The Sahel region is a vast semi-arid strip that stretches across several countries in West and Central Africa. It is characterized by high levels of poverty, inequality, and vulnerability to climate change. The region has a young population, with a slightly higher proportion of men than women. Although high mortality rates of children under five are noted, the region has one of the highest fertility rates in the world, with an average of 5.64 children per woman as of 2019. Life expectancy at birth is around 64.64 years for both sexes.

Regarding the socio-economic situation, countries in the Sahel region share common economic characteristics, yet their national economies vary in size. According to the World Bank classification, the majority of these countries are considered least developed or low-income countries. The region has been described as one of the most underdeveloped regions in the world. Agriculture represents the main sector of employment and value addition, contributing to an estimated 35% of regional GDP.

Despite the Sahel being more effectively affected by socio-economic factors than other regions, it is also characterized by high levels of gender inequality. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes and formal sector activities. According to reports, women provide only 20% of agricultural labor force in the region. The lack of access to education and health services for children, especially girls, is a well-known issue. Women face the greatest challenges in accessing productive resources (land & livestock). Low participation rates among youth are also noted, with young people more often engaged in unpaid household activities rather than productive ones.

The agricultural sector is dominated by women, who make up 80% of the labor force. However, they have limited access to land and other productive resources. Women's participation in the formal economy is low, and their contribution to GDP is estimated at around 35%. The region has a high prevalence of early marriage, with over 40% of girls married before the age of 18. This practice is more common among rural populations.

Regarding the socio-economic situation, countries in the Sahel region share common economic characteristics, yet their national economies vary in size. According to the World Bank classification, the majority of these countries are considered least developed or low-income countries. The region has been described as one of the most underdeveloped regions in the world. Agriculture represents the main sector of employment and value addition, contributing to an estimated 35% of regional GDP.

Despite the Sahel being more effectively affected by socio-economic factors than other regions, it is also characterized by high levels of gender inequality. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes and formal sector activities. According to reports, women provide only 20% of agricultural labor force in the region. The lack of access to education and health services for children, especially girls, is a well-known issue. Women face the greatest challenges in accessing productive resources (land & livestock). Low participation rates among youth are also noted, with young people more often engaged in unpaid household activities rather than productive ones.

The agricultural sector is dominated by women, who make up 80% of the labor force. However, they have limited access to land and other productive resources. Women's participation in the formal economy is low, and their contribution to GDP is estimated at around 35%. The region has a high prevalence of early marriage, with over 40% of girls married before the age of 18. This practice is more common among rural populations.

**Page 190**
---

and profitable investments in intra-African agrarian commerce, regional, and international agricultural value chains.

Deeply rooted in colonialism, patriarchy, and neoliberal capitalism, which have a more pronounced impact on decision-making and leadership positions, laws and policies that regulate formal employment, as well as greater or cultural norms continue to prevent women’s full participation in the economy. This is exacerbated by the lack of gender-based violence at home including sexual harassment and sexual exploitation (UNDP, 2021).

Women’s participation in climate efforts both at the regional and national levels is also very limited. In Malawi, for example, women are excluded from decision-making processes related to agricultural value chains. The patterns of droughts, desertification, and land degradation generally affect women more than men. This is particularly acute in the Sahel due to its high dependence on rain-fed agriculture and therefore on natural resources. Women’s vulnerability to climate change is exacerbated by their lack of access to resources for ensuring food security, nutrition, health, and livelihoods. They are also more vulnerable to extreme weather events that cause extensive crop damage and affect more female farmers who are usually denied access to land and credit (FAO, 2019).

While food production is available for consumption, it remains difficult to a large extent due to the lack of storage and cooling facilities and malnutrition rates very high. At the end of 2022, provisional Celsus humanitarian analyses indicate that 28.9 million people are in need of immediate food assistance, including 14.3 million children. In Ethiopia, the number of people facing severe food insecurity is estimated at 15 million (FEWS NET, 2022). In Kenya, the number of people facing acute food insecurity has increased from 1.8 million to 2.7 million between October and December 2021 due to a combination of factors including conflict, drought, and economic shocks. In Somalia, the number of people in need of humanitarian assistance is estimated at 5.5 million (OCHA, 2022). The situation is exacerbated by the lack of access to food and nutrition services for women and children in Central African Republic, Chad, and Niger.

Despite the African Union’s commitment towards achieving gender equality, women continue to face significant challenges in accessing land, credit, and markets. In many countries across Africa, women represent more than half of the agricultural workforce but have limited access to land ownership and control over productive resources. This is particularly true for women in rural areas who are often denied the right to own land or inherit property from their fathers or husbands.

Women’s participation in decision-making processes related to agriculture and food security is also very limited. In many countries, women are not represented in agricultural value chains and have little say in how resources are allocated and used. This is particularly true for women in rural areas who often lack the education and skills needed to participate effectively in these processes.

Women’s access to credit and financial services is also very limited. In many countries, women are not eligible for loans or other forms of credit from formal financial institutions. This is particularly true for women in rural areas who often lack the collateral required by formal financial institutions.

Women’s access to education and training is also very limited. In many countries, women have limited access to primary and secondary education and therefore lack the skills and knowledge needed to participate effectively in agricultural value chains. This is particularly true for women in rural areas who often lack the resources needed to access education and training.

Women’s access to health care is also very limited. In many countries, women have limited access to reproductive health services and therefore face significant challenges in maintaining their health and well-being. This is particularly true for women in rural areas who often lack the resources needed to access health care.

Women’s participation in decision-making processes related to agriculture and food security is also very limited. In many countries, women are not represented in agricultural value chains and have little say in how resources are allocated and used. This is particularly true for women in rural areas who often lack the education and skills needed to participate effectively in these processes.

Women’s access to credit and financial services is also very limited. In many countries, women are not eligible for loans or other forms of credit from formal financial institutions. This is particularly true for women in rural areas who often lack the collateral required by formal financial institutions.

Women’s access to education and training is also very limited. In many countries, women have limited access to primary and secondary education and therefore lack the skills and knowledge needed to participate effectively in agricultural value chains. This is particularly true for women in rural areas who often lack the resources needed to access education and training.

Women’s access to health care is also very limited. In many countries, women have limited access to reproductive health services and therefore face significant challenges in maintaining their health and well-being. This is particularly true for women in rural areas who often lack the resources needed to access health care.

# Key gender inequalities in the Sahel region

Like many other African countries, gender equality remains a significant issue in the countries of the Sahel region. To gain a better understanding of the situation faced by women in the region, various development partners have conducted surveys to assess the level of gender-based violence and discrimination against women in each country. According to the SDG Gender Index 2021, the average score for all Sahel countries is 58.7 out of 100, which indicates a low level of gender-based discrimination across all four areas examined by the SDG Gender Index. With particular high scores for participation in the family and political spheres and protection against sexual violence, the region's overall score is relatively low due to inequalities in economic empowerment and health.

The discrimination against women within the family ranges from 49% to 83%, based on factors such as early marriage, female genital mutilation (FGM), and child marriage. The level of discrimination against women in the public sphere is also high. The discrimination against women with regard to their economic empowerment ranges from 50% to 76%. In addition, the level of discrimination against women in decision-making positions ranges from 13% to 42%.

Another significant gender indicator is the Global Gender Gap Index, which benchmarks gender parity across four dimensions: economic participation and opportunity, educational attainment, health and survival, and political empowerment. The Sahel region has the largest gender gap in the world, with an average score of 58.7 out of 100. This is significantly lower than the global average of 68.8.

| Chad | 57.6 High | 76% | 0.378 | 58.2 |
| Niger | 57.6 High | 76% | 0.378 | 58.2 |
| Mali | 57.4 High | 79% | 0.391 | 57.9 |
| Mauritania | 57.4 High | 79% | 0.391 | 57.9 |
| Burkina Faso | 56.8 High | 72% | 0.363 | 59.3 |
| Senegal | 56.8 High | 72% | 0.363 | 59.3 |
| Central Africa | 54.1 Low | 65% | 0.311 | 55.1 |

# Socio-economic indicators

In many of the least developed first marriage in the target countries, the percentage of young women (20-24 years) married before age 18 years and women currently, ranging from a very high rate in Niger to a lower rate in Ghana. For instance, the mean age for first marriage is 17 years old, despite the fact that the legal minimum age for marriage is 15 years in Ghana and 18 years in Nigeria. This is similar in many cases the regions are remote to the regulations of force, and the customary and religious laws for marriage prevail.

![Figure 1](image_url)

**Figure 1.** Percentage of young women (20-24) years married before age 18 years old

Furthermore, female genital mutilation has a high prevalence among countries in sub-Saharan Africa. In about 30 countries, it is a common practice in length of governments' efforts to control the practice. In fact, the eight countries with the highest prevalence rates are all in sub-Saharan Africa. This suggests a lack of law enforcement and the cultural preference can restrict people's behavior. In addition, the adolescent fertility rate is another important concern of women's health. The high rate of adolescent fertility is associated with women's health, young pregnant teenagers on women's health, and often on poverty and food security.

There is also a significant gender gap in terms of education. The secondary gender parity index is less than one in all cases, which shows the disadvantage women face relative to men for learning opportunities, especially in sub-Saharan Africa. This is due to the fact that girls are more likely to be excluded from school and the educational system.

# Women’s participation in politics and leadership

Women in West Africa remain underrepresented in the political sphere (Figure 2). Women occupy only 423 seats in West African parliaments, representing 8% of all lawmakers. In West Africa, 1 out of 17 governments is led by a woman. This is a slight improvement from 2015 when women held 6% of the ministerial positions and 9% of the parliamentary seats. Women are also underrepresented in local government bodies, with only 8% of mayors being women.

Women generally occupy subordinate positions, such as secretaries, accountants, human resources, and other administrative positions, and hold few technical and managerial positions. To encourage more women to enter politics, the African Union (AU) adopted the Solemn Declaration on Gender Equality in Africa and the African Charter on Women’s Rights in 2016. This was followed by the adoption of the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (Maputo Protocol) in 2003, which was adopted by the Convention of the African Union.

# Women's Role in Agriculture

Women play an important role in agriculture in the region. However, there are significant disparities from one country to another. For example, in Chad, women account for 78% of agricultural labor but only 10% have access to a bank account. In contrast, in countries where women’s participation in agriculture is lower (e.g., 35% in Burkina Faso), they still face significant challenges such as lack of access to credit and land ownership.

Women dominate employment in off-farm activities, including food away from home, food processing, and small-scale trade. They also play a critical role in the care economy, which is often unpaid but essential for agricultural productivity. Women’s roles are further exacerbated by gender inequalities in rural areas (SWME/DCO2/328). Women face a myriad of obstacles limiting their participation in trade, including lack of access to markets and financial services.

Women also face lower education levels, greater difficulties in accessing credit and property rights, and less access to productive inputs. These challenges are compounded by cultural norms that limit women’s mobility and restrict their opportunities for employment and economic advancement. However, more evidence, strategic thinking, and integrated approaches are needed for women farmers to thrive.

Women’s role in agriculture is crucial for food security and economic development. Despite the challenges, there are opportunities for women to improve their livelihoods through targeted interventions and policies that address the barriers they face.

# Gender and Climate Adaptation

## Climate change is exacerbating gender inequalities.

Gender roles are key to understanding the different ways men and women are affected by climate shocks and stresses. Gender equality is a critical factor in understanding vulnerability and resilience. Women often have less access to resources, information, and decision-making power than men do. The same gendered patterns of inequality that exist in other areas of life also affect adaptation to climate change. There are important gender implications for climate change adaptation.

### First, women are more vulnerable for suffering climate and health-related shocks due to their reliance on natural resources and increased deforestation.

Women's traditional roles as food producers and caregivers make them more vulnerable to climate change. Women are often responsible for collecting water and firewood, which can be affected by changes in weather patterns. Climate change can also increase the risk of disease outbreaks, which can disproportionately affect women and children.

### Second, climate shocks such as droughts and floods are an enormous threat for women farmers.

Women make up 43% of the agricultural workforce globally, but they are more likely to grow crops that are vulnerable to climate change. Women also have less access to credit, technology, and information than men do. In sub-Saharan Africa, for example, women make up 80% of the agricultural workforce, but they own only 1% of the land. This means that women are more likely to be affected by climate change than men.

### Third, as in several African countries, rural women have limited access to agricultural resources and services such as inputs, financial resources, extension services, markets, and credit.

Women often face discrimination when accessing these resources, which can limit their ability to adapt to climate change. Women also face cultural barriers that prevent them from participating in decision-making processes related to climate change adaptation.

### Finally, women are more likely to be affected by climate-related disasters such as floods, droughts, and hurricanes.

Women often have less access to early warning systems and evacuation routes than men do. Women also face cultural barriers that prevent them from seeking help or protection during a disaster.

Moreover, dry spells on natural resources and violence following extreme weather events put women and rural families at risk of food insecurity. At the same time, climate change exacerbates poverty and food insecurity, it can increase competition over resources such as water and land, leading to conflict and displacement.


---
**Page 199**
---

# Climate change impacts on agriculture

Climate change affects agricultural productivity, including crop-based livestock. This can include changes to weather patterns and seasonal variations that affect when crops are planted or harvested. Women play a key role in agricultural activities that are traditionally seen as male domains.

## Women's adaptive capacity

Adapting to climate change depends on people's resilience or coping capacities, which are driven by factors such as access to resources, control over resources, and social networks. As mentioned earlier, women farmers are more likely to have limited access to these resources due to gender-based constraints. Climate change may also be due to gender norms that prevent them from engaging in training and other activities.

Another important factor for adaptability is the time available. Traditional gender roles often mean that women have less time than men to engage in decision-making processes and other activities related to climate change adaptation.

In addition, climate-induced migration in rural areas often affects women disproportionately and their ability to participate in decision-making processes. Women may be more likely to stay behind when male family members migrate due to the belief that women are needed at home. This has consequences for agricultural productivity and food security, as well as gender-based violence. Gender norms also affect women's ability to access credit and influence policies affecting their livelihoods.

Several initiatives have emerged to improve gender equality in a climate change context. A promising approach is to strengthen building blocks on the enabling environment that would allow women to participate more effectively in decision-making processes related to climate change adaptation. These include:

- Increasing women's participation in agricultural activities.
- Promoting gender-responsive agricultural policies and programs.

### Women and land restoration in Isabel region


---
**Page 200**
---

As mentioned in the previous section, the Sahel is characterized by several vulnerabilities to climate change and variability, including droughts, floods, and desertification. The most effective approach to reducing land degradation is to promote sustainable land management practices that improve soil fertility, increase vegetation cover, and enhance biodiversity. Land restoration has been identified as a key measure to boost resilience to flood, drought, and water scarcity. Since women are often responsible for the collection of fuelwood and water, they are also affected by these environmental challenges.

Land restoration programs or initiatives should not be assumed to be gender-neutral; they would benefit from being scrutinized for potential gender-differentiated impacts using a disaggregated data approach. This is particularly relevant when considering the need for land restoration to improve soil fertility, increase vegetation cover, and enhance biodiversity. Women are often responsible for collecting fuelwood and water, which can be affected by environmental degradation.

Women of childbearing age have been identified as a group whose health is directly affected by environmental changes. This is due to their significant involvement in agriculture and food production, reliance on forests, susceptibility to poverty, lack of education, and lower wage employment and social status. Despite the importance of women's roles in land restoration, they are often excluded from decision-making processes related to land management, including soil conservation, reforestation, and preservation, and gender integration is rare.

Including women in land restoration programs provides multiple benefits, such as reducing their exposure to environmental stress, and institutionalizing women's control over natural resources. As in many low-income countries, women's ability to own, control, benefit from, and access land is limited by cultural norms and practices. In the Sahel region, women are often excluded from land ownership due to customary laws that prevent them from inheriting or owning land. Understanding the roles and responsibilities of men and women in land management can help identify opportunities for enhancing gender-responsive and sustainable land restoration.

In addition, customary systems in many societies may prevent women's participation. For example, in some communities, women are not allowed to plant trees on their own land without the consent of their husbands or male relatives. This can limit women's ability to engage in land restoration activities and take measures to improve soil fertility.

## Country Profiles

### Burkina Faso

Burkina Faso is a low-income, agricultural-based country with a high literacy rate and poor housing conditions. It has made significant progress in gender equality over the past decade.

- **Global Innovation Index**: There are deep inequalities between men and women, and social institutions still favor men.
- **Gender Inequality Index (GII)**: Burkina Faso is ranked 147th out of 189 countries.
- **Legal Framework**: The country has adopted laws to reduce social inequalities and increase the number of women in leadership positions.

The Presidential Program also emphasizes the promotion and implementation of women in economic, social, and political life.

Burkina Faso has ratified various international conventions, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). The country has made significant progress in gender equality, but challenges remain. The legal framework protects women's rights, but customary law often overrides these protections.

### Chief

Chief's particular, legal, and socio-economic environment is evolving in line with international, regional, and national commitments to promote gender equality and empower women. The government has established a Ministry for Women's Empowerment (through the Millennium Development Goals, MDGs), and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) have been adopted with the aim of promoting equal opportunities for all citizens.

The government has also implemented programs and initiatives that provide opportunities for women to achieve social and gender equality in rural areas. These efforts include the promotion of women's rights through support under national programs.

However, challenges remain in the Ministry of Women's Empowerment to systematically integrate gender integration across sectors. Performance indicators in sectors relevant to gender equality, such as education and health, are still not fully implemented. Gender issues are often not included in a lack of gender-sensitive policies, inadequate accountability mechanisms, and insufficient resources for implementation.

# Mali

Most of Mali’s population works in agriculture, mostly family and rural farming, including socio-economic entities with family institutions. Family members pool their knowledge, skills, and resources to produce goods that provide a livelihood for several generations.

The Malian government recognizes the importance of the agricultural sector for its increased production, productivity, and decongestion. To support the sector, the government encourages private sector investment and new technologies, land tenure reforms, and develops human capital through education and training in agriculture. The government also supports small-scale irrigation (SAI) and family farming policies.

Mali has included actions to support women’s rights, including legislative, regulatory, and administrative measures that promote gender equality. The country is a signatory in CEDAW and has adopted national laws on gender parity and the elimination of discrimination against women. A specific fund for the Empowerment of Women and the Development of Children (FAD) was also created to promote female entrepreneurship in Mali.

# Mauritania

The 2013 census revealed that the country’s population is characterized by a significant proportion of women, with a percentage of 50.8, but they remain marginalized in the rural areas, particularly in terms of access to land. This situation has led to the perpetuation of cultural practices that reinforce gender inequality and limit women's opportunities for development.

Women in rural areas are tripled to reconstitute families in labor in various sectors of agriculture, trade, and fishing, which leads to their lower participation in decision-making in family and community affairs. In addition, they face difficulties in accessing education and health services. For instance, in Brakna (Southern Mauritania), women represent at most 30 percent of landholders, and only 2 percent are traders. Women who own farms or businesses are rare.

However, agriculture is the main economic activity for rural women in Mauritania, employing more than 80 percent of working women in rural areas. Women participate in all phases of the agricultural cycle, but their work is not recognized, and they do not have access to credit and other financial services. This situation is aggravated by the lack of education and health services, with women and children being more likely to face malnutrition. Agriculture is also influenced by the patterns of natural resources, where a limited number of women have access to land ownership.

Women also play a significant role in activities related to livestock, including traditional transhumance activities, dairy product processing, and small stock breeding. In fact, women make up over 60 percent of those involved in these activities. However, they face numerous challenges, such as lack of access to credit and inadequate infrastructure, which hinders their development.

Another important activity for rural women in Mauritania is the handling and sale of fisheries. They play a significant role in the value chain that starts from fishing and ends with the sale of fish. The processing of fish is also an area where women are actively involved. This includes cleaning, salting, and smoking fish. Households that engage in these activities benefit from the proceeds and potential income generated by these processes.

In the forestry and environmental sector, women are involved in collecting fuelwood and other non-timber forest products. They also participate in the management of natural resources and contribute to climate change mitigation efforts. Women play a significant role in ensuring food security and sustainable development.

Finally, the lack of access to credit, training, basic social services, and financial resources further marginalizes the situation for rural women. The limited access to credit, in particular, leads to their inability to invest in productive activities that could improve their living conditions.

## Niger

According to the government assessment of the agricultural and rural development sectors in Niger in 2019, the country has made significant efforts to promote greater equality and women's empowerment through the adoption of the National Gender Policy (2014-2023) and the National Strategy for Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment. The government has also adopted a student research grant program alongside with the country's Gender Development Index (GDI), which is higher than the sub-Saharan Africa average.

Despite these standard commitments to gender equality in public policies, integrating a gender perspective remains a challenge in agricultural and rural development programs, such as the "Programme de Renforcement des Capacités Institutionnelles et Communautaires pour la Gestion Durable des Ressources Naturelles" (PRCIC-GDRN). # Women's groups and structured agricultural value chains at the local level can help support women's empowerment

Dr. Nnenna Nwakanma is a regional leader of women farming cooperatives with over 40 years' experience. She is from Enugu State, south eastern Nigeria, where women have been farming for centuries. Over 85% of the households in her community are headed by women, who also manage over 20% of the agricultural land. # VI. Sexual Exploitation, Abuse, and Harassment (SEAH)

Sexual Exploitation, Abuse, and Harassment (SEAH) are critical issues affecting women and girls globally, including in the SURAAJA program countries of Burma/Myanmar, Chad, Ethiopia, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria, Senegal, and Uganda. SEAH encompasses a range of harmful behaviors, such as sexual violence, exploitation, and harassment, perpetrated by both private and public persons.

## Global and Regional Context

Globally, approximately 35% of women have experienced either physical and/or sexual intimate partner violence in their lifetime. In some countries, the prevalence of SEAH has been found to be even higher, with estimates reaching up to 70%. In sub-Saharan Africa, the highest rates of SEAH are reported among women in rural areas. In many countries, SEAH is a significant economic impact of HIV/AIDS. The prevalence of SEAH is also influenced by cultural norms and practices.

In sub-Saharan Africa, where the GOM countries are located, the prevalence of SEAH is notably high. For instance, UNICEF reports that in certain regions, up to 50% of women have experienced physical or sexual intimate partner violence. In some cases, this violence is exacerbated by practices such as female genital mutilation (FGM) and early marriage.

## Country-Specific Insights

The following table (Table 3) is meant to provide a snapshot of SEAH-related concerns within the SURAAJA program countries as they pertain to agricultural restoration/NTFP value chains. The table includes information on the prevalence, types, and impacts of SEAH in each country, which will introduce SEAH-specific and cross-cutting information during implementation.

### Table 3: Snapshot of SEAH Across SURAAJA Countries

| Country          | SEAH Types Experienced | Prevalence Rate | Impacts on Value Chains |
|------------------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------------|
| Burma/Myanmar     | Physical and sexual violence, exploitation in agriculture and land restoration. | High (up to 50%) | Reduced productivity, increased dropouts from agricultural programs. |
| Chad              | Sexual harassment and abuse by community leaders and local authorities. | Moderate (20-30%) | Decreased trust in institutions, reduced participation in value chain activities. |
| Ethiopia          | Physical violence and sexual exploitation in NTFP sectors. | High (45-60%) | Disrupted supply chains, increased costs due to absenteeism. |
| Mali              | Sexual harassment by local officials and community leaders. | Moderate (25-35%) | Decreased agricultural productivity, reduced access to markets. |
| Mauritania        | Physical violence and sexual exploitation in agricultural settings. | High (40-50%) | Labor shortages, increased costs for agricultural inputs. |
| Niger             | Sexual harassment by local officials and community leaders. | Moderate (20-30%) | Decreased trust in institutions, reduced participation in value chain activities. |
| Nigeria           | Physical violence and sexual exploitation in agricultural settings. | High (45-60%) | Disrupted supply chains, increased costs due to absenteeism. |
| Senegal           | Sexual harassment by local officials and community leaders. | Moderate (25-35%) | Decreased agricultural productivity, reduced access to markets. |
| Uganda            | Physical violence and sexual exploitation in NTFP sectors. | High (40-50%) | Labor shortages, increased costs for agricultural inputs. |

Note: The prevalence rates are approximate and based on available data from various sources.

# Identifying Potential Risks in These Industries

## Qiblawi
White desert data on SEAL in Qiblawi’s land acquisition and agricultural sectors is limited. The quality of these findings location is a hindrance for agricultural investors. The lack of data on the impact of climate change on agriculture exacerbates the risk of potential SEAL impacts.

## Maan
Maan has a history of human trafficking, with children and women subjected to forced labor and sexual exploitation. The agricultural sector being a significant part of the economy, is susceptible to such abuses, exacerbating the risks of SEAL in this industry. Labor practices are often informal and unregulated.

## Mauntaina
Mountaina has a history of harmful slavery practices, affecting marginalized communities. The lack of data on SEAL impacts makes it difficult to identify the need for targeted interventions to prevent SEAL in agricultural and land sectors.

## Teiger
In Teiger, human trafficking occurs, with children and women exploited in various sectors, including agriculture. The existence of caste-based slavery practices further exacerbates the risks of SEAL in this industry. The lack of data on SEAL impacts makes it difficult to identify targeted interventions.

## Negaria
Negaria recorded World Bank supported Agri-Climate Resilience in Semi-Arid Landscapes (ACR-SAL) projects. About 60% of the population is engaged in agriculture, with about 80% associated with crop activities and livestock. The following “whole of government” approach to SEAL impacts is hindered by lack of data on SEAL impacts. The country’s commitment to sustainable development necessitates the integration of SEAL prevention measures in these industries to mitigate risks.

## Serngei
Serngei has a history of human trafficking, with children and women subjected to forced labor and sexual exploitation. The agricultural sector being a significant part of the economy, is susceptible to such abuses, exacerbating the risks of SEAL in this industry. Labor practices are often informal and unregulated.

## Challenges in Data Collection and Reporting
Accurate data on SEAL is often limited but underreporting, cultural stigma, and inadequate reporting mechanisms hinder effective SEAL risk assessments. The lack of reliable data on SEAL impacts, inconsistent indicators, and different settings, women may be reluctant to report exploitation due to fear of exploitation or lack of trust in reporting systems.

# SEAM Considerations in SIDRAGOWA

SEAM remains a pervasive issue in GGM countries, influenced by various factors including strengthening legal frameworks, enhancing support services for survivors, and promoting gender equality. This chapter builds on previous discussions within the SEAM project and aims to inform Part 2 of this report, as well as the Standalone (Expanded) FAO SEAM Chapter and Chapter 7 of the EAM.

A new set of SEAM-related governance arrangements and GGM referral pathways: IAD ensures that the program remains pertinent and the TIs will be trained on prevention of sexual exploitation, abuse, and harassment. The SEAM project will continue to support and advocate community-driven measures against SEAM. The designation of Gender Focal Points (GFPs) is essential for ensuring that the project can achieve its objectives. The GFPs will be trained in gender-responsive and ethical engagement and grievance mechanisms, while the FAO EIS and Gender Specialist will be engaged in monitoring the process. At SEAM IAG GGM activities will be inclusive, survivor-centered, and gender-sensitive, in case of SEAM.

## VII. Recommendations

### Focusing on Gender-Transformative Approaches

More stakeholders are now recognizing that improving gender equality is a critical component to build shared resilience in the SAEM. A gendered approach to programming can enhance the effectiveness of interventions and support the empowerment of women and girls. Overcoming these challenges requires the promotion of gender-sensitive approaches, including gender-responsive agriculture practices, protection, and enhanced women's participation in decision-making processes. Gender-transformative approaches that increase women's assets, resources, and agency, promote gender-equitable agricultural practices, and enhance women's participation in leadership and decision-making are essential to achieve structural and transformative changes of exclusive shared responsibilities. Development initiatives are engaging to address the approach.

## Approach to Gender Based Violence and Sexual Exploitation and Abuse

While Gender Based Violence and Sexual Exploitation and Abuse are not expected to become the focus of the programme's activities, they are inherent risks when the UNMAS/GGRC countries. Given the, the programme will address issues such as (i) recognition and documentation (ii) clear codes of conduct for staff and partners (iii) reporting mechanisms (iv) referral pathways and (v) support to survivors.

- All grievances of concerned (both in allegations of fraud or other criminal practices, harassment or sexual misconduct) should be reported directly to the Gender Action Plan (GAP) focal point. The focal points will be designated by each country (this will be done at a higher authority level within the country-specific Gender Action Plan). The focal points will be responsible for ensuring that all grievances are addressed in accordance with the S/2016/248 guidelines and the UN Secretary-General's Bulletin on Special Measures for Sexual Misconduct (ST/SGB/2003/13). A short summary of how SIA processes will be managed is below.

### Gender Action Plan (GAP)

The programme-led GAP builds on the findings and recommendations of the Gender Assessment. The developed Gender Action Plan was presented, the GAP provides specific activities under each component to assist teams in building their capacity to create change and move towards enhanced gender equality. The Gender Action Plan will be implemented by the UNMAS/GGRC countries, particularly with the focus on women, demonstrated NTFP values, character and leadership skills, and the ability to work effectively within a team. The Gender Action Plan will be updated annually to reflect changes in the programme and related GAP have been developed.

Please refer to the [Code of Conduct for the United Nations](https://www.un.org/en/sections/code-of-conduct/index.html) for full details.

### Country-led Gap

Each account for different countries context, a quality-based GAP will be prepared the National Social Development (NSD) and the UNMAS/GGRC countries. The assistance will be aligned with the programme's GAP and further align at country-level based on the local context.

Please refer to the [Gender Action Plan](https://www.un.org/en/sections/gender-action-plan/index.html) for full details.
